are launching the [FASHN agent skill](https://docs.fashn.ai/getting-started/agent-skill), a new way to help coding agents understand how to integrate FASHN into your own projects.\n\nInstead of pasting API docs into every chat, you can install the skill once and ask your agent to add FASHN features directly to your codebase. The skill teaches agents how to use the FASHN REST API, the official [TypeScript SDK](https://docs.fashn.ai/sdk/typescript), and the [Python SDK](https://docs.fashn.ai/sdk/python), including the prediction lifecycle, polling, response formats, errors, webhooks, and endpoint inputs.\n\nIt works with Claude Code, Codex, Cursor, and other coding agents that support skills.\n\n\u003cVideo src=\"https://static.fashn.ai/website/blogs/introducing-the-fashn-agent-skill-for-coding-agents/fashn-claude-code.mp4\" /\u003e\n\n---\n\n## Install the skill\n\nInstall the skill with [skills.sh](https://www.skills.sh/):\n\n```bash\nnpx skills add fashn-ai/fashn-skill\n```\n\n\u003e **Having trouble?** Ask your coding agent to help you install the skill. It can check whether Node.js, npm, or `npx` is available on your machine and suggest the right setup steps for your environment.\n\nThe CLI auto-detects supported coding agents on your machine and configures the skill for each one.\n\nThe skill reads your FASHN API key from `FASHN_API_KEY`. If the variable is not set, the skill can ask for your key and help configure it for future runs. You can generate a key from the [FASHN API setup page](https://docs.fashn.ai/getting-started/api-setup).\n\nFor project integrations, we recommend adding the key to your local environment file:\n\n```bash\nFASHN_API_KEY=your_api_key_here\n```\n\nKeep it server-side. Do not expose it with public environment variable prefixes such as `NEXT_PUBLIC_`.\n\nIf Claude Code or Codex is already open when you install the skill, restart the session so the agent can load the new instructions.\n\n---\n\n## Why this matters for project integration\n\nFASHN integrations usually touch more than one file. A production-ready virtual try-on or product-to-model feature often needs:\n\n- A secure server-side place to call the FASHN API\n- A `FASHN_API_KEY` environment variable that never reaches the browser\n- Input handling for model, product, pose, or reference images\n- A polling or subscription flow for long-running generations\n- Error handling that maps API failures to useful UI states\n- Optional webhooks, scripts, or background jobs for automated workflows\n\nThat is exactly the kind of work coding agents are good at, but only if they have the right context. The FASHN skill gives them that context before they start editing your app.\n\nFor example, you can point Claude Code or Codex at a Next.js, React Router, Django, FastAPI, Laravel, or custom backend project and ask for a specific feature:\n\n```text\nUse FASHN to add a virtual try-on flow to this project.\nAccept a model image and a product image, call FASHN server-side,\nand return the generated result URL to the UI.\n```\n\nThe agent can inspect the project structure, choose the right integration path, install the SDK when appropriate, add the backend call, and connect it to the existing UI conventions.\n\n---\n\n## Using the skill in Claude Code and Codex\n\nAfter installation, open Claude Code or Codex in your project and mention FASHN in your request. Be specific about the product behavior you want, not just the endpoint name.\n\nBoth agents can inspect your repository, identify the framework, and choose between REST, the TypeScript SDK, or the Python SDK based on what the project already uses. In a TypeScript app, that often means installing `fashn` and using `client.subscribe(...)` so the server-side code submits the job and waits for the final result. In other backends, REST may be the better fit.\n\nFor a Next.js app:\n\n```text\nUse the FASHN skill to add product-to-model generation to this Next.js app.\nCreate a server action that accepts a product image URL and returns the generated on-model image URL.\n```\n\nFor a Python worker:\n\n```text\nUse the FASHN skill to add a Python script.\nRead product images from ./input-products, generate on-model images with FASHN,\nand write the results to ./output-results.\n```\n\nClaude Code and Codex can also run quick one-off generations by invoking the skill directly:\n\n```text\n/fashn generate three on-model shots of this product for our product page\n```\n\n```text\n/fashn do a try-on with this model photo and this jacket\n```\n\nFor one-off generations, make sure the agent can actually access the image inputs. In Codex Desktop, you can drag and drop images into the thread and reference them in your `/fashn` request. In Claude Code, point the agent to a local file path instead, for example:\n\n```text\n/fashn do a try-on with ./images/model.jpg and ./images/jacket.png\n```\n\n{/* TODO: Add Codex Desktop demo video here. */}\n\nOne-off runs are great for testing prompts or assets, but persistent integrations are where the skill saves the most time: adding real FASHN-powered features into an app you already maintain.\n\n---\n\n## What to ask the agent to build\n\nThe best prompts describe the user workflow and the project constraints. Here are a few good starting points.\n\n### Virtual try-on\n\n```text\nUse FASHN to add virtual try-on to this app.\nUsers should upload a model photo and a product image.\nCall FASHN only from server-side code, poll until the generation is complete,\nand display the result image in the existing UI.\n```\n\n### Product-to-model\n\n```text\nUse FASHN to add product-to-model generation for our catalog workflow.\nAccept a product image, generate multiple on-model outputs,\nand store the returned image URLs using the existing database pattern.\n```\n\n### Bulk Product to Model script\n\n```text\nUse FASHN to build a bulk product-to-model script.\nRead input image URLs from a CSV, submit jobs with FASHN,\nhandle failures, and write output URLs plus credit usage to a results file.\n```\n\n---\n\n## Review checklist for generated integrations\n\nEven with a good skill, you should review the generated code before merging. For FASHN integrations, check that:\n\n- `FASHN_API_KEY` is read only from server-side code\n- The key is not logged, returned to the client, or committed to source control\n- The integration uses the same framework conventions as the rest of the project\n- Long-running jobs use `subscribe` or a clear `run` plus `status` polling flow\n- User-facing errors are helpful without exposing internal API details\n- Image inputs are validated before sending requests\n- Scripts include retry and failure handling if they run unattended\n- Credit usage and generated output URLs are stored where your product needs them\n\nThe skill can get you most of the way there. Your project knowledge still matters for permissions, billing rules, storage, and the exact user experience.\n\n---\n\n## Start building\n\nInstall the skill:\n\n```bash\nnpx skills add fashn-ai/fashn-skill\n```\n\nThen open Claude Code or Codex in your project and ask for the feature you want to ship.\n\nFor more details, read the [FASHN coding agents documentation](https://docs.fashn.ai/getting-started/agent-skill) or explore the [skill repository](https://github.com/fashn-AI/fashn-skill).\n"])</script><script>self.__next_f.push([1,"1a:T432a,"])</script><script>self.__next_f.push([1,"\n## Introduction\n\nWhen we started building subscriptions for our iOS app, the app was not meant to be a separate product. It was an extension of our existing platform.\n\nOur existing platform already had a web app, a Next.js backend server (backend), and a Postgres database where we keep account, subscription, billing cycle, and credit state. On the web app, subscriptions are handled through Stripe. The iOS app had to plug into that system instead of creating a second source of truth.\n\nThat meant subscriptions had to work across web and mobile. A user with an existing web subscription should be able to open the iOS app and keep using the product. A user who subscribed on iOS should have the same access on the web app. The subscription state, billing cycle, and credit behavior all had to stay in sync across both platforms.\n\nAdding the paywall UI sounded like a weekend project. It was not. The paywall UI itself was the easy part. The hard part was everything behind it: connecting mobile purchases to existing FASHN accounts, keeping billing state consistent with the web app, and making sure subscription events updated credits at the right time.\n\nIn this post, we walk through the architecture, implementation decisions, and edge cases that shaped the final system, including why we removed annual plans from mobile entirely to avoid a class of crossgrade problems we did not want to deal with.\n\n## Goals\n\nHere's a list of what we needed this system to support:\n\n- Allow users to purchase a subscription from the mobile app using Apple IAP\n- Let users access the mobile app with their web subscription and vice versa\n- Sync subscription data in our backend\n- Listen to subscription events to offer, revoke and update credits\n\n## Architecture\n\nWe use [RevenueCat](https://www.revenuecat.com/) to manage subscriptions and RevenueCat webhooks to keep our backend in sync.\n\nWe chose RevenueCat because we did not want to build and maintain the Apple subscription plumbing ourselves, especially knowing that we plan to roll out an Android app in the future. It gives us one place to configure products and offerings, handles receipt validation, exposes subscription state through an API, and sends webhooks for lifecycle events like purchases, renewals, cancellations, expirations, and product changes. It also gives us room to use built-in paywalls and A/B testing later if we decide to move more of the paywall experience into RevenueCat. That means we do not have to model every difference in subscription behavior across iOS and Android ourselves, and can focus instead on how those events should affect FASHN accounts, credits, and cross-platform access.\n\nWhenever we receive a webhook from RevenueCat, we consume that event and create an async task using [Vercel Workflows](https://vercel.com/workflows), which is our async task runner.\n\nInside that task, we inspect the event type and process it accordingly.\n\nFor every event, we start with the same basic flow:\n\n1. Sync the subscription data in our database\n2. Decide whether the event should change the user's credits\n\nThis split made the system much easier to reason about. All events are used to sync subscription data, but only a few relevant events are used to work on credits. Some events only update billing or access state, while others grant, reset, or revoke credits.\n\nThis architecture also gave us one backend controlled system that could support both iOS and web instead of treating them as two separate subscription products.\n\n## Implementation\n\nNow that the high-level flow is in place, the next step is explaining how each goal was achieved in practice.\n\n### Setting up products in Apple and RevenueCat\n\nBefore any code, the subscription products need to be created in both Apple and RevenueCat.\n\nIn App Store Connect, subscriptions are organized into subscription groups. All plans that a user can upgrade or downgrade between must live in the same group, because Apple uses the group structure to determine what counts as an upgrade, downgrade, or crossgrade. Within a group, each subscription is assigned a level. The level controls how Apple ranks the plans relative to each other.\n\nWe have one subscription group with three plans:\n\n| Level | Name   | Duration |\n| ----- | ------ | -------- |\n| 1     | Agency | Monthly  |\n| 2     | Pro    | Monthly  |\n| 3     | Basic  | Monthly  |\n\nLevel 1 is the highest tier. A user moving from Basic to Pro is an upgrade. A user moving from Agency to Basic is a downgrade.\n\nIn RevenueCat, we create products that map to the Apple product IDs. These products are then grouped into an offering, which is what the app fetches to display the paywall. We do not use RevenueCat's built-in paywall UI. Instead, we fetch the offering and render a custom paywall using a manual implementation.\n\n### Cross-platform subscriptions\n\nOne of the main goals was allowing users to move between iOS and web without losing access to their subscription.\n\nTo make this work, we did not treat the iOS app as a standalone subscription environment. Instead, we kept the subscription state in our backend and synced it using RevenueCat events.\n\nThat meant a user with a web subscription could log into the iOS app and continue using it, and a user who subscribed on iOS could use the same subscription on the web app.\n\nThe important part here was that our backend became the place where we determine the latest subscription state. The iOS app can read subscription state from the RevenueCat SDK APIs, but that client-side state is not the source of truth for our product. Our backend is what both web and mobile rely on for access and credit decisions.\n\n### Syncing subscription data\n\nAnother goal was to keep subscription data in sync in our backend.\n\nSubscriptions are not useful to us if they only exist inside Apple or RevenueCat. We need that data in our own database for a few concrete reasons:\n\n- We track the billing cycle so we know when to reset credits\n- We track subscription status to unlock paid features for active subscribers\n- We record cancellations so we can show users the date their access ends\n- We keep this state in our backend so both web and mobile can read from the same source\n\nWithout this, we would have no reliable way to communicate subscription state to users, support access across platforms, or gate features.\n\nWe keep this data updated by listening to RevenueCat webhooks. That is why every RevenueCat event goes through the same sync flow. Whether it is an initial purchase, renewal, cancellation, expiration or product change, we first update the subscription state in our database. After that, only the events that affect credits continue into the credit-handling flow.\n\nThis gave us a single source of truth that both platforms could use.\n\n### Acting on subscription events\n\nAnother goal was listening to subscription events not just for syncing, but also to decide what should happen to credits.\n\nIn our app, subscriptions are tied to credits. So a subscription event is not just a billing event. It is also a product event.\n\nFor example:\n\n- On purchase, we offer credits for the subscribed plan\n- On renewal, we reset credits for the new billing cycle\n- On cancellation, we update the subscription state\n- On expiration, we revoke the relevant credits\n\nThis was one of the main reasons we needed reliable webhook handling. We were not just mirroring billing state. We were acting on it.\n\nWe also make webhook processing idempotent so retries do not grant or reset credits twice.\n\n### Initial purchases, renewals and expiration\n\nInitial purchase events are straightforward but important.\n\nWhen a user purchases a plan successfully, we update the subscription data in our database and then grant credits based on the purchased plan.\n\nRenewals were especially important because our credits reset with the billing cycle. So when a renewal happens, we do not increment credits from the previous month. We refresh the credit balance for the new billing cycle.\n\nFor example, if a user is on Basic and had 100 credits left from the previous cycle, we do not add another 200 and make it 300. We simply refresh the balance and give them a fresh 200 credits for the new cycle.\n\nCancellation and expiration also needed different handling. When a cancellation event comes in, we update the subscription status in the database, but we do not revoke credits immediately because the subscription is still active until the current billing period ends. When the expiration event arrives, that is when we revoke the relevant credits.\n\n## Challenges\n\nThere were a few challenges that made this project more complex than a normal subscription integration.\n\n### Keeping subscription data in sync\n\nThe first challenge was keeping subscription data in sync across Apple, RevenueCat, our backend and our app.\n\nWe addressed this by routing every RevenueCat webhook through the same backend workflow and making our database the canonical state used by both web and mobile.\n\n### Handling upgrades\n\nBefore going into how we handled these, it helps to understand [how Apple defines subscription changes](https://developer.apple.com/app-store/subscriptions/#ranking).\n\n\u003e **Upgrade.** When a user switches to a higher-tier subscription, the change is immediate. Apple refunds the prorated amount of their original subscription.\n\u003e\n\u003e **Downgrade.** When a user switches to a lower-tier subscription, the current subscription continues until the next renewal date and then renews at the lower level and price.\n\u003e\n\u003e **Crossgrade.** When a user switches to a subscription at the same level, the timing depends on the billing frequency. If both subscriptions are the same duration, the change is immediate. If the durations are different, the new subscription takes effect at the next renewal date.\n\nUpgrades were a challenge because they are immediate changes.\n\nWhen a user upgrades, the new subscription starts right away with an updated billing cycle. This means we cannot treat upgrades like renewals or cancellations. They need their own handling because the subscription state changes immediately.\n\nSince Apple refunds the prorated amount of the original subscription, we also have to prorate the credits we offer. A user who upgrades mid-cycle should not receive the full credit allocation for the new plan. They should only receive credits proportional to the time remaining in the billing cycle.\n\n```\n// Step 1: Calculate the refund Apple will give for the current plan\ncurrent_plan_refund = current_plan_price × (days_remaining / days_in_cycle)\n\n// Step 2: Calculate the net amount the user is paying for the new plan\nnet_payment = new_plan_price - current_plan_refund\n\n// Step 3: Calculate the proportion of the new plan they're paying for\npayment_ratio = net_payment / new_plan_price\n\n// Step 4: Award credits proportionally\nnew_plan_credits = new_plan_total_credits × payment_ratio\n```\n\nThis would have been simpler if Apple offered an option to turn off proration on upgrades. In that case, we could have just offered the full credit allocation for the new plan and carried over any remaining credits from the old plan. But since the effective payment is prorated, we have to prorate the credits to match.\n\nAny remaining credits from the old plan are carried forward as top-up credits. That way, the user's unused credits are not lost, while the new subscription credits still reflect the prorated amount they paid for the upgraded plan.\n\nFor upgrades, RevenueCat sends both a `PRODUCT_CHANGE` event and a `RENEWAL` event, since the new billing cycle starts immediately.\n\nWe handled this by treating product changes as their own event path in the workflow instead of trying to force them into the renewal logic.\n\n### Apple account vs app account\n\nThis is only a problem if your app requires users to have an account. If it does not, subscriptions are simply tied to the Apple account and there is no mismatch to worry about.\n\nSince our app requires an account, we ran into this. Subscriptions on iOS are tied to the Apple account, not to the account the user logs into inside our app.\n\nA user can have one phone but multiple accounts with us. For example, they may have one account created with Gmail and another created with GitHub.\n\nThat means a user could purchase a subscription while using one account, then later log into the app with a different account on the same phone. Even though the subscription exists on their Apple account, it does not automatically belong to every account they have with us.\n\nWe had to handle this case carefully. When a user tries to subscribe while logged into a different app account, RevenueCat returns a `RECEIPT_ALREADY_IN_USE_ERROR`. We catch that error, detect that a subscription already exists but is linked to another account, and show that to the user instead of creating a broken state.\n\n### Renewal timing\n\nSyncing renewals was also more challenging than expected.\n\nWe receive a `RENEWAL` event from RevenueCat, but the subscription data in RevenueCat is not always updated at that exact moment. RevenueCat says this happens because Apple can send the renewal event before the actual renewal datetime.\n\nSo when we receive the `RENEWAL` event, we first fetch the latest subscription state from the RevenueCat API and compare it against what we have in our database. If the `current_period_starts_at` and `current_period_ends_at` values are the same as what we already have, it means RevenueCat has not updated yet.\n\nIf the data is updated, we process the renewal normally.\n\nIf it is not updated yet, we schedule a task to run just after the `current_period_ends_at` timestamp. That gives RevenueCat enough time to reflect the renewed subscription, and we fetch the updated state then.\n\n### Offering both monthly and annual plans\n\nAnother source of complexity was offering both monthly and annual plans on mobile.\n\nInitially, we had annual plans:\n\n1. Agency (Monthly/Annual)\n2. Pro (Monthly/Annual)\n3. Basic (Monthly/Annual)\n\nAgency is the highest tier and Basic is the lowest.\n\nSame-tier changes were not the main problem. A move like Basic monthly to Basic annual is a crossgrade, but in our setup those crossgrades always changed billing duration, so they would take effect at the next renewal date.\n\nThe harder problem was upgrades that also changed billing frequency. For example, moving from Basic annual to Pro monthly is still an upgrade, but it also changes the subscription duration. That combination made timing and credit proration harder to reason about and easier to get wrong.\n\nAnnual plans also added another backend path for credit resets. For monthly plans, the credit reset can be tied directly to the `RENEWAL` webhook event. For annual plans, the user only renews once a year, but we would still need to reset subscription credits every month according to that user's billing cycle. That is doable with a cron job or workflow, but we did not want to add that extra backend scheduling logic in the first version.\n\nAt first, we thought about simply removing the annual options from our paywall. But that would not be enough, because users can still manage subscriptions directly through Apple Settings and trigger those changes there.\n\nTo avoid this complexity, we decided to remove annual plans from the mobile subscription group altogether.\n\nAfter that, the subscription changes we needed to focus on were much simpler because there were no billing frequency changes anymore:\n\n1. Upgrades\n2. Downgrades\n\n## Summary\n\nOverall, the project was less about adding a paywall and more about building a reliable subscription state machine around Apple, RevenueCat and our own backend.\n\nThe hardest parts were keeping subscription data in sync, handling the mismatch between Apple accounts and our app accounts, processing renewals only when RevenueCat data was actually ready, and avoiding the complexity of crossgrades and mixed frequency upgrades.\n\nIf you are building subscriptions for a cross-platform product, we strongly recommend deciding early how subscription events will map into your own backend state and how those events should affect the rest of your product, especially if subscriptions also control credits or other consumable limits. If that is the case, it is also worth considering offering only monthly plans. Mixing billing frequencies introduces crossgrade scenarios and complicates upgrade proration, because a user changing both their plan level and billing frequency at the same time makes credit calculations significantly harder to reason about and get right.\n\n## Resources\n\n- [RevenueCat](https://www.revenuecat.com/)\n- [Vercel Workflows](https://vercel.com/workflows)\n- [RevenueCat webhook event flows](https://www.revenuecat.com/docs/integrations/webhooks/event-flows)\n- [RevenueCat configuring products](https://www.revenuecat.com/docs/projects/configuring-products)\n- [RevenueCat connect a store](https://www.revenuecat.com/docs/projects/connect-a-store)\n- [RevenueCat Apple server notifications](https://www.revenuecat.com/docs/platform-resources/server-notifications/apple-server-notifications)\n- [Apple App Store subscriptions](https://developer.apple.com/app-store/subscriptions/)\n"])</script><script>self.__next_f.push([1,"1b:T305a,"])</script><script>self.__next_f.push([1,"\n## TL;DR\n\n**What's new?**\n\nGoogle Research and UW introduced **FIT**, a 1.13 million-sample virtual try-on dataset with ground-truth body and garment measurements, plus a companion model called **Fit-VTO**.\n\n**Why does it matter?**\n\nToday's virtual try-on is good at helping shoppers visualize style, but falls short on sizing. FIT is the first serious attempt at try-on that reflects real fit, closing in on the original promise of reducing returns.\n\n**What's actually out there now?**\n\nThe [paper](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.08526) and [project page](https://johannakarras.github.io/FIT/) are live; the dataset and code are marked \"coming soon.\" Fit-VTO is a research prototype, not something brands can deploy today.\n\n**What to expect for the future?**\n\nOur guess? Probably a hybrid model. Today's virtual try-on would remain the default because it is low-friction for both shoppers and retailers. Fit-aware virtual try-on would become an opt-in upgrade when shoppers and retailers can share measurements.\n\n## The year VTO became mainstream\n\nVirtual try-on has been technologically possible for years, but 2026 is when it began to move from an experimental add-on to a standard part of online apparel shopping. A clear sign was [Zara's rollout of virtual try-on](/blog/how-to-add-virtual-try-on-to-your-store-like-zara). After a quiet start in select markets through late 2025 and a Spain-first launch in January, Zara expanded the feature globally in March, turning it on in-app for one of the largest shopper bases in fashion. At FASHN AI, we've seen interest from name brands shift from exploratory conversations to near-term deployment planning.\n\n![Image](https://static.fashn.ai/website/posts/zarap-app.png)\n\n\u003cFigcaption\u003eZara's virtual try-on feature in their mobile app.\u003c/Figcaption\u003e\n\nPart of why VTO spread this fast is that the prerequisites for integration are minimal. You need a product image (already on the product page) and a user image (provided by the shopper). No retailer schema changes, no catalog re-tagging, no data-sharing agreements. The AI does the rest.\n\n![Image](https://static.fashn.ai/website/posts/tryon-example.png)\n\n\u003cFigcaption\u003e\n  A virtual try-on result using the FASHN API: a product image and a user photo\n  are the only inputs needed.\n\u003c/Figcaption\u003e\n\nAnd the experience is genuinely good for what it does. Today's generative image-editing models can take your photo and a product image and produce a clean, plausible visualization of you wearing the item. That makes current VTO great for styling, inspiration, and mixing and matching. It is the digital equivalent of holding something up against yourself in the mirror.\n\n## But VTO hasn't solved the problem it was invented for\n\nThe original pitch for virtual try-on was not \"help shoppers imagine an outfit.\" It was \"reduce returns.\" The numbers help explain why. [Apparel remains one of the highest-return categories in retail](https://www.shopify.com/enterprise/blog/fashion-conversion-rate-optimization), and [39% of consumers who return apparel say fit is the reason](https://www.powerreviews.com/research/apparel-shopping-trends-2023/merchandise-returns/). The garment arrives, it doesn't sit the way the shopper expected, back it goes. Virtual try-on was supposed to help prevent that kind of fit-driven return.\n\nCurrent VTO still falls short on that goal. The models powering today's try-on experiences are generative image editors trained to produce plausible, aesthetic outputs. Given a person and a garment, they generate a clean, well-fitted visualization regardless of whether the garment would actually fit that person. A size M shirt and a size XL shirt on the same shopper look broadly similar in the simulation, even though they would feel noticeably different in real life.\n\n![Image](https://static.fashn.ai/website/blogs/google-research-and-uws-fit-what-it-means-for-virtual-try-on/tryon-triplet.webp)\n\n\u003cFigcaption\u003e\n  A try-on result from the FASHN API. The output looks natural and well-fitted,\n  but that does not guarantee the garment would actually fit this way in real\n  life.\n\u003c/Figcaption\u003e\n\n## Why nobody built a fit-aware VTO dataset until now\n\nIf the fit gap is so well-known, why hadn't anyone built a measurement-rich dataset years ago? Three main challenges.\n\n**Scale.** Modern generative models generally need training data in the millions of samples. That alone is a big undertaking.\n\n**Triplet curation.** To learn real try-on, a model needs three images: the product image of the target garment, an image of the person wearing that garment, and an image of the same person wearing something else in the same pose. There is no natural e-commerce workflow that creates that data at scale, so researchers have to synthesize it or approximate it from simpler pairs. Making those synthetic triplets realistic enough to transfer back to real photos is a hard problem. We ran into the same issue at FASHN and addressed it with a synthetic triplet pipeline in [FASHN v1.5](/research/vton-1-5).\n\n**Measurement annotation.** This is the hardest part. A fit-aware model needs ground-truth measurements for every body and every garment in every sample. Collecting that from the real world means deep cooperation with shoppers willing to share exact body measurements and retailers willing to share precise per-garment specs beyond marketing S/M/L labels. Neither side has a strong natural incentive to contribute. This is the core reason fit-aware datasets did not exist at this scale until now.\n\n## What Google + UW just built\n\n[FIT](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.08526) is a two-part release from researchers at the University of Washington and Google Research. The dataset contains 1.13 million virtual try-on triplets, each annotated with ground-truth measurements for both the body and the garment. It is the first dataset of its kind at this scale. The companion model is **Fit-VTO**, a diffusion-based try-on model that uses those measurements at generation time.\n\n![Image](https://static.fashn.ai/website/blogs/google-research-and-uws-fit-what-it-means-for-virtual-try-on/fit-teaser.webp)\n\n\u003cFigcaption\u003e\n  FIT dataset overview: layflat garment images (top), person images (middle),\n  and try-on results (bottom) across a range of body and garment sizes, from\n  tight fit to loose fit. Each sample includes precise body and garment\n  measurements. Source: [FIT project\n  page](https://johannakarras.github.io/FIT/).\n\u003c/Figcaption\u003e\n\nThey avoid the measurement-collection problem by generating the dataset synthetically. Parametric 3D garments are built and dressed onto parametric 3D bodies, then a physics engine simulates how each garment drapes. Because everything upstream is parametric, the measurements are ground-truth _by construction_. That removes the need for cooperation or data-sharing agreements. A re-texturing step converts the synthetic renders into photorealistic images while preserving the simulated geometry, and a paired-person step produces the same identity wearing two different garments so the full triplet structure is intact.\n\nAt a glance:\n\n- 1,137,282 training triplets + 1,000 test samples\n- 168 body shapes (XS–3XL) in 528 poses\n- 158,483 unique upper-body garments\n- Seven body measurements (bust, waist, hips, height, shoulder, length, sleeve) and three garment measurements (width, length, sleeve), all derived from the physics simulation\n\nSynthetic data alone wasn't sufficient to train the model, though. The authors report that pure-synthetic training didn't transfer well, and they had to mix in roughly 330,000 real online fashion images (without measurement labels) to get the model to generalize. The final recipe is synthetic for ground-truth measurements, real imagery for photographic realism.\n\n![Image](https://static.fashn.ai/website/blogs/google-research-and-uws-fit-what-it-means-for-virtual-try-on/fit-size-progression.webp)\n\n\u003cFigcaption\u003e\n  Fit-VTO results: same person, same garment, four different sizes (XS, M, XL,\n  3XL from left to right). The model generates different drape and fit for each\n  size rather than defaulting to a single well-fitted result. Source: [FIT\n  project page](https://johannakarras.github.io/FIT/).\n\u003c/Figcaption\u003e\n\n## The honest caveats\n\nFIT is a major step, but it is still a research release, not a production-ready system.\n\n**Tightness granularity is unsolved.** The physics engine distinguishes loose from fitted, but not tight from very tight. Two garments that are both smaller than the person's body size tend to render with similar tightness, even if one should look noticeably tighter than the other.\n\n**Tops only, front-facing poses only.** The entire release is constrained to upper-body garments in casual front-facing poses. Pants, dresses, multi-layer apparel, and complex poses are listed as future work.\n\n**No human preference study.** The paper evaluates with image-similarity metrics but doesn't publish a study of whether real shoppers actually judge fit-aware outputs as better. For a system intended to improve trust in fit, that is an important missing evaluation.\n\n## What this means for virtual try-on going forward\n\nFIT does not change what brands can deploy tomorrow. What it changes is the target. Until now, most virtual try-on systems have been judged mainly on whether the result looks plausible. FIT makes room for a more demanding question: does the garment appear to fit this specific body in a believable way?\n\nThat does not mean today's low-friction version of VTO goes away. It works because it asks for very little: a product image and a shopper photo. A fit-aware system would ask for more from both sides. Shoppers would need to share body measurements, and retailers would need to provide per-garment specifications that go beyond standard size labels.\n\nFrom our perspective at FASHN AI, the most likely outcome is not that fit-aware VTO replaces standard VTO, but that the two coexist. Standard VTO would remain the default for styling, inspiration, and quick purchase decisions. Fit-aware VTO would become an additional mode for cases where both the shopper and the retailer are willing to provide the data. If that model matures, the harder question will be when the added accuracy justifies the extra friction.\n\n## Bonus for the technically curious\n\nA few findings from the paper that don't show up on the project page but are worth surfacing if you care about how this is built.\n\n**Using measurements as a text prompt reduces fit accuracy substantially.** The obvious productization shortcut would be to feed measurements as natural-language tokens (\"chest 38, waist 32, length 27\") through a standard text encoder and let the existing cross-attention do the rest. The authors tested it. Fit accuracy dropped sharply in the ablation. Pre-trained text encoders aren't designed to represent structured numerical inputs.\n\n**Normal maps preserve simulated geometry in the photorealistic output.** The re-texturing step (synthetic render → photorealistic image) is the most fragile part of a synthetic-first pipeline. It's where simulated geometry usually gets washed out by the photorealism model. The authors use a [FLUX.1 dev model](https://huggingface.co/black-forest-labs/FLUX.1-dev) conditioned on surface normal maps of the simulated scene. Because normals encode geometry directly, the photorealistic output preserves the simulated drape and fit, rather than \"improving\" it into a generic good-looking result.\n\n**Heads and feet are inpainted separately.** Synthetic bodies often have characteristic artifacts: bald avatars, weird toes. The authors route heads and feet through a second inpainting pass with a Gemini 2.5 image model, recompute surface normals on those regions, and stitch them back into the main image before the re-texturing step runs.\n\n## Closing\n\nVirtual try-on went mainstream in 2026. FIT is the first large-scale, measurement-aware dataset aimed at closing the fit gap. Its existence should push future work toward benchmarks that measure fit directly. The [paper](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.08526) and [project page](https://johannakarras.github.io/FIT/) are live today; the dataset and code are expected to follow, and the work will be presented at [SIGGRAPH 2026](https://s2026.siggraph.org/).\n\nFor brands looking to add virtual try-on to their store today, [FASHN AI](/virtual-try-on) is a good place to start.\n"])</script><script>self.__next_f.push([1,"15:[\"$\",\"$L18\",null,{\"posts\":[{\"slug\":\"introducing-the-fashn-agent-skill-for-coding-agents\",\"frontmatter\":{\"title\":\"Introducing the FASHN Skill for Coding Agents\",\"slug\":\"introducing-the-fashn-agent-skill-for-coding-agents\",\"summary\":\"The new FASHN skill helps Claude Code, Codex, Cursor, and other coding agents integrate FASHN into real projects with less documentation handoff.
